# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-29

## Multimodal Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[MultiFair: Multimodal Balanced Fairness-Aware Medical Classification with Dual-Level Gradient Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07328v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical decision systems increasingly rely on data from multiple sources to ensure reliable and unbiased diagnosis. However, existing multimodal learning models fail to achieve this goal because they often overlook two critical challenges. First, various data modalities may learn unevenly, thereby converging to a model biased towards certain modalities. Second, the model may emphasize learning on certain demographic groups causing unfair performances. The two aspects can influence each other, as different data modalities may favor respective groups during optimization, leading to both imbalanced and unfair multimodal learning. This paper proposes a novel approach called MultiFair for multimodal medical classification, which addresses these challenges with a dual-level gradient modulation process. MultiFair dynamically modulates training gradients regarding the optimization direction and magnitude at both data modality and group levels. We evaluate MultiFair on three real-world medical classification datasets with diverse demographic attributes,including multiclass classification and missing-modality settings. Experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This work has been accepted for publication in IEEE Transactions on Medical Imaging</p></details> |
| **[Peers4U: Designing Learner-Centered Virtual Peers for Children's Multimodal Mathematics Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37238v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In early mathematics, children learn abstract concepts through concrete representations, such as manipulating objects, drawing, and speaking. Learning with peers can enrich this process by exposing children to diverse strategies and enabling them to exchange ideas through interaction. We realized this potential with virtual peers in Peers4U, a multimodal learning system to enhance children's mathematical conceptual understanding. Virtual peers driven by LLMs are personalized to the learner's cognitive level and assigned multimodal solutions with inspectable processes. During learning, peers engage in mixed-initiative interactions by responding, initiating topics, and advancing discussions along a pedagogical progression. A user study with 22 children and two experts showed our learner-centered topic orchestration significantly outperformed a persona-centered baseline, and children spontaneously authored new artifacts to demonstrate inspired ideas or share their own strategies, producing significantly more solutions when grouped with more Leading Peers. We contribute design insights for multimodal, learner-centered virtual peer systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[ML-ECS: Heterogeneity-Aware Collaborative Multimodal Learning for Edge-Cloud Synergies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14107v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Edge-cloud synergies provide a promising paradigm for privacy-preserving deployment of foundation models in heterogeneous web environments, enabling lightweight edge models to support domain-specific multimodal we b services while cloud-hosted models facilitate collaborative knowledge sharing. However, in practical edge-cloud web environments, collaborative multimodal learning is challenged by modality heterogeneity (different modality combinations across domains) and model-structure heterogeneity (different modality-specific encoders/fusion modules). To address these issues, we propose ML-ECS, a collaborative multimodal learning framework that enables joint training between a server-based model and heterogeneous edge models. This framework consists of four components: (1) cross-modal contrastive learning (CCL) to align modality representations in a shared latent space, (2) adaptive multimodal tuning (AMT) to preserve domain-specific knowledge from local datasets, (3) modality-aware aggregation (MAA) to robustly aggregate while mitigating noise caused by missing modalities, and (4) SLM-enhanced CCL (SE-CCL) to facilitate bidirectional knowledge transfer between cloud and edge. Experimental results on various multimodal tasks show that ML-ECS consistently outperform state-of-the-art baselines under varying modality availability, achieving improvements of 1.55% to 16.75% in Rouge-LSum and improving both client- and server-side performance. In addition, by communicating only low-rank LoRA parameters and fused representations, ML-ECS achieves high communication efficiency, requiring only 0.71% of the total parameter volume.</p></details> |  |
| **[VaViT: Vanilla ViT for Automotive Point Cloud Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31177v2)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Plain Transformers have become the de-facto architecture for processing text, audio, image, and video, offering a unified backbone for multimodal learning. However, state-of-the-art architectures for point cloud semantic segmentation remain dominated by U-Nets architectures where convolutions are interleaved with local or windowed attentions. In this work, we show how to effectively leverage vanilla, non-hierarchical ViTs for the segmentation of large-scale automotive lidar scenes. We bridge the performance gap thanks to a carefully designed tokenizer, a lightweight decoder segmentation head, and tailored data augmentations. Our approach, VaViT, for Vanilla ViT, matches or exceeds the performance of state-of-the-art methods while maintaining the simplicity of ViT architecture. We provide extensive evaluations on nuScenes, SemanticKITTI, and Waymo Open Dataset to validate the efficiency of our method. Code and pre-trained models are available at https://github.com/valeoai/VaViT.</p></details> | WACV 2027 |
| **[Ampe-DB: A Dataset for Human Activity and Interaction Analysis Using the Ghanaian Game Ampe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33130v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human activity recognition benchmarks underrepresent culturally situated forms of movement and interaction. We introduce Ampe-DB, a multimodal dataset based on Ampe, a traditional Ghanaian game involving coordinated jumping, leg movements, and two-person interaction. It contains 637 canonical clips from 15 participants, with RGB video, two-person pose representations, participant and pair metadata, four-class movement labels, and derived binary movement-relation labels. We evaluate four-class movement recognition and binary movement-relation classification using three protocols: a duplicate-safe fixed benchmark, four-fold pair-held-out evaluation, and participant-held-out diagnostics for participants with complete class coverage. Baselines comprise a frozen Kinetics-400-pretrained R(2+1)D-18 RGB encoder, a two-layer bidirectional GRU for pose sequences, and validation-selected RGB-pose late fusion. For four-class recognition, RGB macro-F1 falls from 0.955 on the fixed benchmark to 0.418 under pair-held-out evaluation and 0.384 under participant-held-out evaluation. Pose is more robust, achieving 0.957, 0.811, and 0.839 across the same protocols. Fusion reaches 0.976 on the fixed benchmark and 0.824 under pair-held-out evaluation, but does not consistently improve on pose under participant shift. Binary movement-relation recognition also becomes substantially harder under held-out evaluation, especially for different-leg configurations. These findings show that strong fixed-benchmark scores can mask failures to generalize across people and pairings. Ampe-DB supports research on fine-grained movement recognition, human-human interaction, multimodal learning, and identity-aware generalization.</p></details> | <details><summary>74 pa...</summary><p>74 pages, 9 figures, 7 numbered tables. Ampe-DB V1 dataset (637 interaction clips) available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/RuthBiney/Ampe-DB</p></details> |
| **[Reliability-Aware Probabilistic Fusion for Multimodal Learning with Incomplete Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29621v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal inputs may be missing or degraded, raising distinct questions of evidence availability and trust. We introduce a reliability-aware Gaussian Product-of-Experts (PoE) in which an input-dependent reliability weight tempers each modality's latent precision, while missing experts are omitted. We characterize how reliability and availability affect posterior information and expert influence. Across two clinical multimodal tasks, the full framework remains competitive with complete inputs and achieves its clearest mean-AUROC advantage under modality absence, particularly on HAIM-MIMIC-MM. Ablations show a substantial gain from missing-aware training and an additional HAIM improvement from reliability weighting under matched PoE, missing-aware, and consistency training. Unseen test-time corruptions are used diagnostically and reveal modality-dependent reliability-weight and latent-uncertainty responses, including failure under severe degradation. Thus, reliability weighting provides an explicit evidence-control mechanism, while learning dependable quality signals remains an open challenge.</p></details> |  |
| **[On the Complementarity of Quantum and Classical Features: Adaptive Hybrid Quantum-Classical Feature Fusion for Breast Cancer Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22903v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of quantum machine learning with classical deep learning offers promising avenues for medical image analysis by mapping data into high-dimensional Hilbert spaces. However, unifying these paradigms remains challenging due to optimization asymmetries. We propose a hybrid quantum-classical architecture for breast cancer diagnosis based on a dual-branch feature-extraction pipeline. Our framework unifies complementary representations from classical models and trainable or deterministic (non-trainable) quantum circuits. Three fusion strategies are introduced: Static Hybrid Fusion (SHF) for offline extraction, Dynamic Hybrid Fusion (DHF) for end-to-end co-adaptation, and Temperature-Scaled Hybrid Fusion (TSHF). Inspired by multimodal learning, TSHF uses a learnable scalar to balance branch gradient dynamics and address optimization bottlenecks. We evaluate BreastMNIST, INbreast, and BUS-UCLM, spanning ultrasound and mammography, using ResNet-18 and a shallow CNN, with five independent runs per configuration. Fusion improves over the strongest classical baseline on all three benchmarks. On BreastMNIST, TSHF with ResNet-18 and a non-trainable circuit achieves 88.33% accuracy, 92.09% F1-score, and 92.09% AUC-ROC, versus 84.74% accuracy and 87.08% AUC-ROC for ResNet-18 alone. On INbreast, SHF with the same pairing performs best, at 70.42% accuracy, 72.96% F1-score, and 80.28% AUC-ROC. On BUS-UCLM, SHF with a trainable circuit achieves 78.46% accuracy and 84.66% F1-score, over seven accuracy points above the best standalone branch. Adaptive fusion is not universally beneficial: static fusion performs best on the two smaller datasets, suggesting an additional estimation burden from co-adaptation and learned calibration under limited data. Clinical applicability requires validation beyond the small, low-resolution datasets and simulated quantum circuits considered here.</p></details> | <details><summary>41 pa...</summary><p>41 pages, 16 figures. This manuscript is a preprint under review at Artificial Intelligence in Medicine</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Invariance: Factor-wise Selective Treatment of Shifts for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21403v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Distribution shifts pose a fundamental challenge to robust multimodal learning. Existing methods typically promote invariance by suppressing environment-dependent variations, implicitly applying a uniform treatment to shifted information. However, shifted information can have different predictive consequences: some contributes little to prediction, some remains reliably informative, and some exhibits environment-dependent predictive effects. This raises a fundamental question: which shifted information should be suppressed, preserved, or attenuated? To address this question, we propose FaShift, a factor-wise shift framework that diagnoses latent representation factors according to their distributional changes, intervention effects, and cross-environment stability. By factorizing multimodal representations and evaluating these complementary properties, FaShift distinguishes non-predictive factors, stably predictive factors, and predictively useful but unstable factors. It then selectively suppresses, preserves, or attenuates their contributions, rather than enforcing uniform invariance. Experiments on MOSI, MOSEI, and CH-SIMS show that FaShift achieves the best ID accuracy and F1 across all three datasets. Compared with the strongest competitors reporting OOD results, it improves OOD accuracy/F1 by 1.5/3.6 points on MOSI and 1.7/2.7 points on MOSEI, while achieving the best reported OOD accuracy and F1 on CH-SIMS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reusing Multimodal Agents: Efficient Evaluation of Sound Designs in Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27670v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper introduces a novel framework for efficiently evaluating in-game sound designs using multimodal learning and reinforcement learning. Sound design significantly influences gameplay and accessibility; however, objective evaluation methods, defined here as automated assessments based on agent performance rather than human ratings, often require retraining agents from scratch for each sound design. Our approach first pre-trains a multimodal encoder that encodes visual and auditory information into a shared latent space. A reinforcement learning agent is then trained using this latent representation. We fine-tune the multimodal encoder for each sound design while keeping the trained policy fixed, allowing us to isolate the effects of sound changes on agent behavior. To evaluate the proposed method, we conducted experiments on games from multiple genres, comparing the performance of blind agents (agents using only sound input) under various sound designs. Experimental results suggest that this approach can detect differences in agent performance caused by degraded or altered sound design. Moreover, we significantly reduce the computational cost compared to retraining the agent from scratch. Experimental results suggest that this approach provides a practical and scalable solution for evaluating in-game sound designs. The source code, data and supplementary document are available: https://github.com/thainv0212/tog-reusing-agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[VLX-Go: Robust Embodied Tracking through Multi-Stage Learning and On-Policy Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19361v2)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to identify a designated target from visual observations and control its own motion to follow that target through an environment. Following an isolated person is a comparatively simple demonstration of embodied visual tracking, but following the intended person among distractors remains challenging. Existing methods exhibit a substantial drop in success from single-target to distractor tracking, motivating a focus on the shared spaces that person-following robots must navigate. We present VLX-GO, a vision-language waypoint policy built around the hypothesis that visual and language representations should be aligned before tracking-specific motion is learned. The policy combines complementary frozen visual encoders, multiscale temporal context, a transformer backbone, and a lightweight head for robot-centric waypoint prediction. Its training progresses through vision--language alignment, multimodal reasoning mid-training, and tracking specialization on 19,532 oracle episodes. Compared with 41.54% distractor-tracking success for our earlier OmTrackVLA model, the complete multi-stage recipe achieves 55.80%. We then explore on-policy refinement using filtered oracle labels for states visited by the policy. This preliminary study adds 2.42 percentage points of distractor-tracking success, reaching 58.22%, while reducing collision rate by 1.99 pts. The final policy records the highest distractor- and ambiguous-tracking success rates among the compared methods. These results support staged multimodal learning as a promising direction for tracking among distractors and motivate further study of on-policy refinement for person following in shared spaces. Code is available at https://github.com/om-ai-lab/VLX-Go.</p></details> | tech report |
| **[CheXFuse: Guided Differential Joint Attention Fusion for Robust Multimodal Chest X-Ray Classification in Data-Scarce Medical Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25617v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central challenge in multimodal learning is how to fuse modalities that differ substantially in predictive strength. When one modality provides a much stronger representation than another, indiscriminate cross-modal interaction can introduce weak, redundant, or unstable signals, limiting or even degrading performance relative to the strongest unimodal representation. To address this problem, we propose CheXFuse, a multimodal fusion framework based on Guided Differential Joint Attention (GuDJA). CheXFuse combines image and text token representations within a joint multimodal sequence, where learned token guidance emphasizes informative representations and differential attention reduces less useful attention responses. We evaluate CheXFuse on the Indiana University (IU) Chest X-ray dataset, using binary (normal vs. abnormal) classification as the downstream task. Experiments are conducted under both original-report and masked-report settings, with the latter serving as a controlled stress test under reduced textual informativeness. CheXFuse with GuDJA achieves the strongest mean overall performance across the evaluated baselines and ablations, reaching 95.73% accuracy, 95.79% F1-score, 96.54% recall, and 95.28% sensitivity at 95% specificity (Sens@95Spec) with original reports, and 95.58%, 95.60%, 95.60%, and 94.34%, respectively, under masked reports. These results suggest that GuDJA provides a promising fusion strategy under asymmetric or partially reduced modality informativeness.</p></details> | IEEE ICDM DMBIH 2026 |
| **[MM-LeJEPA: Correlated Gaussian Regularization for Multimodal Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24681v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LeJEPA derives an isotropic Gaussian embedding target by minimizing, under its task assumptions, a Fisher information criterion that controls the task-averaged squared prediction bias. This enables unimodal self-supervised learning without reconstruction decoders, exponential-moving-average teachers, or contrastive negatives. However, its isotropic task prior does not account for the cross-modal structure of multimodal tasks that read shared content. We introduce MM-LeJEPA by extending LeJEPA's task assumptions to multimodal learning. Specifically, we model multimodal tasks as strength-weighted combinations of components that read independent information from the two modalities and components that read the same content from both. Under explicit gradient-moment, correspondence, and symmetry assumptions, we derive a correlated Gaussian target that minimizes a weighted Fisher information functional under a fixed total variance constraint. An analytic transform adapts Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularization (SIGReg) to this target, yielding a two-term objective that combines distributional regularization with local-to-global prediction. The extension adds a single scalar hyperparameter, the target cross-modal correlation, and retains the simplicity of LeJEPA. On public audio-visual classification benchmarks, our results are competitive with representative open-source methods. Code will be released.</p></details> |  |
| **[AdamPM: Memory-Efficient LLM Optimization via Moment Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24598v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Adam is widely used for training large language models (LLMs) owing to its strong optimization performance and training stability, but its element-wise estimation for the first and second moments incur substantial memory overhead. Although numerous memory-efficient optimizers have been proposed, many incur additional computational overhead or require additional tuning of hyperparameters. Consequently, Adam remains a dominant and reliable choice for LLM pre-training. We propose AdamPM (Adam with Pooled Moments), a simple and memory-efficient variant of Adam that groups adjacent moment entries along the row or column dimension and represents each group by a single shared mean. AdamPM can well inherit Adam's hyperparameter settings and is readily integrated into existing Adam optimizer implementations, with less memory overhead. We systematically investigate pooling of both first and second moment estimates and identify second moment pooling as the effective design. With a group size of $s$, AdamPM reduces second moment memory to $1/s$ of the original requirement. Extensive experiments on language model pretraining, fine-tuning and multimodal learning demonstrate that AdamPM maintains model performance and training speed while substantially reducing optimizer-state memory. Our code is available at \url{https://github.com/jixiaowei95/AdamPM}.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[MSCR: Jointly Balancing Utilization Across Modalities and Discovering Synergistic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24395v1)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In multimodal learning, modality imbalance often lets one modality dominate optimization and keeps the model from fully exploiting cross-modal interactions. Balance-oriented methods such as the Multimodal Competition Regularizer (MCR) perform well by alleviating this imbalance during training. These dynamic balancing methods, however, mainly adjust modality contributions and do not consider how imbalance affects the formation of synergistic information during fusion. Synergy is a major source of multimodal advantage, because it is predictive gain that cannot be recovered from unimodal evidence alone. We propose the Multimodal Synergistic Competition Regularizer (MSCR), a fusion method that jointly promotes balanced modality utilization and the discovery of synergistic information. MSCR has two complementary objectives. An anti-dominance objective suppresses excessive unimodal influence and leaves room for synergistic information, and a synergy objective encourages the fused predictor to outperform a non-synergistic unimodal reference. An adaptive gate further concentrates the regularization on samples that need stronger collaborative modeling. Experiments on five benchmarks show that MSCR consistently improves performance over MCR and strengthens synergistic capability. It raises conditional recovery, the accuracy of the fused prediction on samples that the unimodal predictors misclassify, as well as the share of the model's output that InterSHAP attributes to cross-modal interactions. Its gain in conditional recovery persists when the fusion module is Transformer-based. Code is available at https://github.com/chenxinyu991-collab/MSCR.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Cattle-CLIP: A Multimodal Framework for Dairy Cattle Behaviour Recognition from Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09203v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Monitoring cattle behaviour provides valuable insights into animal health, productivity and welfare. However, robust behaviour recognition in real-world farm environments remains challenging because well-annotated livestock video datasets are scarce and agricultural surveillance footage differs substantially from large-scale pretraining data. To address these challenges, we propose Cattle-CLIP, a domain-adaptive vision-language framework based on Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP), which reformulates cattle behaviour recognition as a cross-modal semantic alignment task. Cattle-CLIP incorporates a temporal integration module that extends image-level contrastive pretraining to video-based behaviour understanding and promotes consistent semantic alignment across time. To reduce domain discrepancy, customised data augmentation and behaviour-oriented prompts are employed. We also construct CattleBehaviours6, a curated dataset containing 1,905 annotated clips from six indoor behaviours: feeding, drinking, standing-self-grooming, standing-ruminating, lying-self-grooming and lying-ruminating. The dataset provides standardised ethogram definitions for model training, evaluation and future livestock behaviour research. Cattle-CLIP was evaluated under supervised and few-shot learning settings, with particular emphasis on data-constrained behaviour recognition. In supervised experiments, the model achieved 96.1% accuracy across six behaviours and showed high precision and recall for feeding, drinking and standing-ruminating. It also maintained promising few-shot performance with limited labelled data, demonstrating the potential of multimodal learning for cattle behaviour recognition in data-constrained farm environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[Pathway-Structured Privileged Distillation for Deployable Computational Pathology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02877v3)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Integrating transcriptomics and histopathology can improve cancer risk modelling, yet practical use is constrained by the limited availability of RNA profiling in routine settings. Here we introduce Mixture of Pathway Experts (MoPE), a knowledge-distillation framework that reframes multimodal learning as privileged distillation for histology-only inference. MoPE is motivated by the partial observability between RNA profiles and whole-slide images: histology can capture morphology-linked consequences of certain molecular programmes, but cannot be expected to reconstruct the full transcriptomic state. MoPE encodes RNA-derived pathways and transfers the molecular supervision to pathway-indexed pathology experts through memory-usage alignment. Across diverse public benchmarks and two independent breast cancer cohorts, MoPE consistently improved WSI-only inference performance relative to baseline methods. Pathway-usage analyses and human-audited visual inspection provide bounded inspection of model behaviour and candidate morphology-linked readouts. These results support pathway-structured privileged distillation as a promising route to using molecular information during training while preserving RNA-free inference.</p></details> |  |
| **[CIIR - Class-Indexed Interface Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23151v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal learning commonly assumes paired or synchronized observations, yet independently collected datasets rarely provide such correspondence. We introduce CIIR, a modular encoder--decoder framework that connects heterogeneous datasets through a class-indexed address space while preserving each dataset's native input format and output module. A known local index selects address $k$, and an input-dependent 128-dimensional address representation Z_k(x) is written to that address. To demonstrate CIIR, we learn this address space from handwritten digits, clothing images, spoken-digit spectrograms, and hand signs without synchronized or instance-matched samples. Newly trained label-free receivers recover the requested addresses with 94.7% macro-average Address Realization Accuracy. We then attach the unseen SVHN dataset after freezing the base address space and train only lightweight adapters, achieving 90.2% MNIST>SVHN and 82.3% SVHN>MNIST end-to-end Address Transfer Accuracy without target-side decoder labels. To demonstrate generalizability of CIIR, we reuse the ten addresses as code elements in collision-free arithmetic and hierarchical address codes for all 100 CIFAR-100 classes and evaluate direct arithmetic-code realization through the existing receivers. These results establish CIIR as a compact interface for modular cross-dataset communication and post-hoc dataset attachment without exhaustive paired multimodal supervision.</p></details> | 17 pages, 6 figures |
| **[TWIN-MoE: Two-stage Intra-Expert Imputation Network for Incomplete Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24014v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches address missing modalities either by routing each missingness pattern to a dedicated expert, which scales poorly as the number of modalities grows, or by imputing the missing modality from representations contributed by multiple experts, which requires a costly retrieval mechanism. We show that routing induces expert-conditioned representation neighborhoods and derive an imputation-error bound controlled by their local feature dispersion. When these expert-conditioned neighborhoods are more compact than the global representation space, intra-expert aggregation admits a tighter worst-case reconstruction bound than unconstrained global aggregation. Guided by this analysis, we propose TWIN, a two-stage intra-expert imputation framework. Pre-imputation generates a context-conditioned estimate in the encoder embedding space and radially soft-clips it using modality-specific statistics maintained in an observed FIFO queue, this ensures that the pre-imputed modality stays in empirically calibrated region of the target modality space. Post-imputation refines the missing representation after MoE by aggregating expert-conditioned observed representations within the same sample. Across four missing-modality benchmarks under natural, random, and asymmetric dropout protocols, TWIN achieves state-of-the-art performance while using substantially fewer parameters than competing MoE-based imputation methods.</p></details> |  |
| **[When to Align, When to Predict: A Phase Diagram for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11190v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-modal alignment (CA) and cross-modal prediction (CP) are the dominant paradigms for multimodal representation learning, yet there is no systematic understanding of when each succeeds and when each fails, a gap that leaves practitioners, especially in scientific domains with heterogeneous instruments and multiple levels of measurement, unable to diagnose why standard methods underperform the best single modality. We study both objectives under a spiked signal-plus-noise model with structured cross-modal nuisance correlation, the ingredient that breaks the classical recovery guarantees, and derive separation ratios that expose complementary failure modes: alignment whitens each modality and fails when nuisance is strongly correlated across views; prediction encodes whatever is cross-predictable through a one-sided whitening, with recovery governed by source-modality quality. The resulting phase diagram partitions multimodal problems into four regimes --- Both, CA only, CP only, and Neither --- refined by a recovery count that separates partial recovery from complete failure. We present a data-driven procedure to locate real-world datasets in this diagram using a small labeled subsample, identifying the preferred objective and prediction direction before any cross-modal training, and identifying when no objective in the CA/CP family can improve on the stronger modality alone. Experiments on synthetic data, stereo-vision benchmarks, image--caption pairs, and two real scientific domains, astronomy and single-cell multi-omics, validate the predictions in the nonlinear regime, including both faces of the Neither regime. Code to reproduce the results is available at https://github.com/IlayMalinyak/mm_align_vs_pred.</p></details> |  |
| **[Task-Centric Multimodal Learning for Cognitive Impairment Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20906v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dementia affects millions of older adults worldwide, yet early detection remains difficult outside clinical settings. Traditional models primarily rely on text or voice-based responses, capturing only a limited view of human cognition despite the inherently multimodal nature of human expression. This work proposes a contactless, multimodal screening framework that leverages video, audio, and text signals collected during structured voice-based interactions with Amazon Alexa. By incorporating these modalities, our approach captures a more holistic representation of human behavior encompassing speech patterns, vocal characteristics, facial expressions, and linguistic structure, which may enable more accurate detection of cognitive decline. Different participants completed three conversational tasks designed to probe spontaneous speech, comprehension, and recall. We pursued three objectives: (1) evaluate which of the three Alexa tasks provides the most informative cognitive signals, (2) compare the effectiveness of unimodal models, and (3) assess the benefits of multimodal fusion models. Task-level analysis shows that the video model achieves its best performance on Command-Reading, with an accuracy of 0.65 (AUC 0.69), while the audio model performs best on Question-Answering, reaching an accuracy of 0.67 (AUC 0.73). In contrast, the multimodal model demonstrates consistently stable performance across tasks, achieving an accuracy of 0.76 (AUC 0.75) on Command-Reading and 0.76 (AUC 0.73) on Question-Answering. These findings demonstrate that multimodal behavioral cues captured through naturalistic interactions can reliably distinguish MCI from healthy aging, highlighting the limitations of traditional diagnostic paradigms and supporting the shift toward multimodal, real-world cognitive assessment frameworks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the 2026 IEEE Global Communications Conference (GLOBECOM)</p></details> |
| **[Instruction Mining From Images: Constructing a Synthetic Dataset for Multimodal Learning ILSP DCVLR Competition Submission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19932v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work presents a compact and reproducible pipeline for synthesizing and curating image-based instruction-response pairs for DCVLR. We draw from the Cauldron collection and use Magpie-style instruction and response synthesis with Qwen2.5-VL-7B Instruct to create concise, image-conditioned instruction pairs. The pipeline consists of instruction synthesis, conditioned on input images and controlled via a system prompt, sanitization, tagging, and filtering of the instructions based on the score obtained by a reward model (DeBERTa-v3). We retain samples with positive scores to maximize the quality of the generated instructions. We observe that score distributions have a median and mean reward score of ~ 1.53 and ~ 1.75 respectively, indicating that most generated instructions are of good quality. The resulting dataset contains diverse instructions in the math, spatial, and social reasoning domains.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Modality-Agnostic Seizure Classification: A Unified Deep Learning Framework for Heterogeneous EEG and Intracranial EEG Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16596v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Seizure prediction and detection, essential for clinical treatment of epilepsy, remain the crucial task of this neurological disease. EEG and iEEG are widely used to analyze epilepsy due to its capability of capturing discriminative brain activities at high time resolution. However, existing works generally analyze either EEG or iEEG seizure activity separately, where EEG analysis has been well-researched, while more and more works consider iEEG for its higher specificity at the spatial level and signal quality. Currently, most of the existing seizure prediction systems are single-modal in nature, which limits its application to a variety of clinical conditions. Therefore, a common framework to analyze seizures with both EEG and iEEG signals needs to be developed. This paper presents a new framework of multimodal seizure classification integrating the heterogeneous EEG and iEEG recordings from different subjects and sources. A specific framework, called Unified Multimodal Signal Harmonization (UMSH), is developed for harmonizing signals with varied sampling rates and recording conditions. Moreover, to handle the extremely class-imbalanced problem in the data sets, we use a Conditional Generative Adversarial Network (cGAN) based data augmentation technique to generate synthetic samples to balance the class distribution. | **[Do More Modalities Always Help? | **[Preference vs. Performance: EEG-Based Classification of Learner Engagement in Multimodal Instruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05178v1)** | 2026-10-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective adaptive instructional systems require robust measures of learner engagement that go beyond static user profiles. | **[InstMoE: Adaptive Multimodal Routing with Specialized Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05111v1)** | 2026-10-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal inputs are inherently heterogeneous, not only across modalities but also in the information pathways required for effective prediction. | **[Continual Learning with Elastic Regularization and Synthetic Replay for Federated MLLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.12112v2)** | 2026-10-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated fine-tuning of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) across distributed networks enables privacy-sensitive adaptation to evolving data streams, yet a fundamental obstacle prevents robust deployment in dynamic environments: catastrophic forgetting, wherein sequential task updates erase previously acquired knowledge across visual, linguistic, and cross-modal representations. LID shows task-dependent relationships with performance, while RankMe provides the strongest measure for classification and clustering, but is not a universal layer selector. Based on this insight, we propose multimodal Max Confidence Regularization (MaxCR) to dynamically intervene in modality semantic confidence. Specifically, the semantic confidence of each modality is tracked using a nonlinear sparsity measure. We then design max suppression and max excitation based on this measure to regularize strong and weak modalities, respectively. They penalize and encourage the top-1 confidence, thereby constraining multimodal prediction. To this end, strong and weak modalities are expected to make calibrated confidence, thereby improving the overall performance. Specifically, VCMM estimates minibatch noise and temporal drift online and uses their relative strength to determine modality-specific momentum through a Kalman-inspired controller. We further center the control signal across modalities and apply exact bias correction for the time-varying first moment, enabling adaptive gradient memory without extra network passes or explicit learning-rate scaling. | **[From Models to Systems: A Comprehensive Survey of Efficient Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19445v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid expansion of multimodal models has surfaced formidable bottlenecks in computation, memory, and deployment, catalyzing the rise of Efficient Multimodal Learning (EML) as a pivotal research frontier. This survey systematizes the EML landscape by introducing the first structured, model-to-system taxonomy. We distill insights from over 300 seminal works into three hierarchical levels--model, algorithm, and system--addressing architectural parsimony, execution refinement, and hardware-aware orchestration, respectively. Moving beyond a purely categorical review, we offer a methodological synthesis of the vertical synergies between these layers, elucidating how cross-layer co-design contributes to the fundamental "Efficiency-Utility-Privacy" trade-off. Through an integrative case study of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), we trace the field's evolutionary trajectory from initial structural adjustments to modern full-stack resource orchestration. Furthermore, we provide a holistic discussion and application-specific optimization blueprints for diverse domains and posit a paradigm shift toward self-regulating intelligence, where efficiency is an intrinsic, emergent property of the model's fundamental design rather than a post-hoc constraint. Finally, we present open challenges and future directions that will define the trajectory of EML research. This survey establishes a structured framework for multimodal systems that are not only high-performing and generalizable but natively efficient and ready for ubiquitous deployment. Mutual Information (MI), the Information Bottleneck (IB), and Partial Information Decomposition (PID) provide complementary perspectives, yet existing studies often treat them as isolated tools. This survey presents an information-theoretic perspective connecting these principles as progressively refined views of multimodal information processing: MI characterizes inter-modal dependency, IB explains task-oriented information preservation under compression, and PID decomposes preserved information into redundancy, uniqueness, and synergy. We review 170 recent studies (2018--2026) and 12 foundational works, organizing multimodal learning around four challenges: cross-modal alignment, information-efficient fusion, interaction-type characterization, and scaling to multimodal foundation models. Rather than using application domains as primary taxonomy axes, we interpret healthcare, robotics, recommendation systems, affective computing, and wireless communications as empirical validations of these information principles. Beyond taxonomy, we organize existing multimodal paradigms within a single information-theoretic coordinate system -- the Generalized Multimodal Information Lagrangian -- in which they occupy exact or approximate parameter corners, and whose unoccupied regions name candidate method families the literature has not yet built. We further discuss how emerging multimodal foundation models instantiate these principles at scale and identify open challenges including scalable information estimation in high-dimensional settings, standardized evaluation across information-theoretic methods, combinatorial complexity of multimodal PID, and the transition from post-hoc information analysis toward information-aware multimodal learning.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-GS: Balanced Multimodal Learning via Calibrated Gating and Fusion Surgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24947v3)** | 2026-09-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>End-to-end training of multimodal neural networks often exhibits unstable neural dynamics characterized by three coupled failure modes that degrade learning: (i) modality imbalance, where one branch dominates gradient-based optimization; (ii) unstable gating, where noisy confidence cues induce erratic modality selection; and (iii) fusion interference, where modality-specific gradients conflict at the shared fusion layer. | **[Semantics at an Angle: When Cosine Similarity Works Until It Doesn't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16318v4)** | 2026-08-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cosine similarity is a standard comparison rule for learned representations in information retrieval, natural language processing, computer vision, and multimodal learning. Its popularity is well founded: it removes positive radial scale, is computationally convenient, and often matches objectives that train normalized embeddings. These same properties also delimit what cosine can express. Normalization discards radial variation; anisotropic representations can compress angular contrast; high-dimensional neighborhoods can develop hubs; and a symmetric, uncalibrated score may be mismatched to the relation of interest. This article offers a selective review organized around a simple principle: the usefulness of cosine similarity depends jointly on the learned representation, any normalization or post-processing, the scoring rule, and the downstream decision. We derive the main geometric identities, distinguish failure mechanisms that are often conflated, review representative evidence about embedding norms, and describe objective-matched, geometry-aware, hubness-aware, norm-aware, and learned alternatives. ## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Ego-CEVR: Learning Cross-Embodiment Visual Representations from Human Egocentric Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37509v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As egocentric video becomes increasingly available for embodied learning, an important research question is how to turn this low-cost, scalable source of data into robot manipulation capabilities. We present Ego-CEVR, a data-native framework for hierarchical visual representation learning that assigns distinct learning objectives to global and interaction representations. The global module learns object relations, task state, and temporal progress through future prediction on egocentric video. The Interaction module interprets wrist observations in global context and learns local alignment and contact cues through robot action supervision. Separate residual paths expose both representations to the action policy, preserving direct access to task context alongside fine-grained execution information. Ego-CEVR supports independent pretraining of its global video module on large-scale human egocentric videos and videos from diverse robot embodiments, without requiring robot action labels or paired wrist observations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Twincher: Towards Robust and Precise Physical AI via Bijective Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13470v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Perception and planning in physical systems often require inverting a continuous forward map $p \mapsto y = f(p)$, e.g., a renderer or a simulator, that can be queried but not inverted in closed form. Learned inverses are limited by their approximation error, whereas iterative solvers in observation space can stall at spurious stationary points. We instead learn a representation $r(y)$, insensitive to perturbations of $y$ normal to the data manifold, in which $p \mapsto r(f(p))$ is a well-conditioned bijection. In these coordinates, the least-squares inverse problem has no spurious interior stationary points, so that Gauss-Newton iteration with the exact forward model converges to the accuracy the data permit; the learned model need only be qualitatively correct. We introduce Twincher, an exactly invertible architecture of localized area-preserving rotations, trained with Jacobian-level objectives and a domain-growing curriculum. On synthetic maps with tunable nonconvexity, the worst-case residual reaches machine precision within five iterations once the query budget suffices, whereas an MLP-based baseline improves only gradually. In 6D localization of industrial objects in real depth images of the MVTec ITODD benchmark, given instance masks, twinchers trained only on CAD renderings make the iteration converge from a fixed initial guess within a few steps, without feature matching, pose regression, or pose hypotheses; in observation space, the same iteration fails for up to half of the views. All validation poses pass the strictest BOP thresholds and fit the sensor data about as closely as the ground-truth poses; 80\% of all 239 instances (67\% with visible masks only) are solved in agreement with the sensor data, and the number of training iterations grew only 1.5-fold from two to four pose parameters. Code is open source.</p></details> |  |

For informative neighborhoods, we show that expectile aggregation can exploit class-dependent scale differences to achieve a classification advantage over mean aggregation. Under a local asymptotic regime with diminishing per-neighbor label informativeness, we establish that this advantage can persist in terms of limiting classification risk. (ii) It incorporates a training and adaptation strategy that enables high-resolution PDE solution generation from sparse input grids. (iii) It adopts a generative modeling paradigm that learns complete trajectory segments, mitigating long-horizon error drift. | **[Is High Local Accuracy Enough? To alleviate this, we develop a novel multi-marginal (MM) inverse OT (IOT) contrastive learning (CL) approach called Neg-MMIOT-CL, which learns representations such that the global multi-marginal OT (MMOT) coupling between a triple of data marginals, with respect to a carefully designed ground-cost between triplets of data points in the representation space, concentrates on the anchor-positive-negative $\textit{triplets}$. For a latent class model, we empirically show that Neg-MMIOT-CL alleviates dimensional collapse. Furthermore, for a specific choice of ground cost for all triplets in representation space, we prove that the optimal representation configuration for Neg-MMIOT-CL exhibits equiangular property for within-class and across-class representations, which translates to Neural-Collapse when the representation dimension is larger than the number of classes minus one -- a result that is $\textit{previously established only}$ for pairwise contrastive learning methods. Finally, we propose Neg-IOT-CL-PushPull, that is a computationally efficient alternative to Neg-MMIOT-CL, alleviating the high cost of computing MMOT plans needed during implementation. ## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[A Unified Three-Stage Weighting Framework for Causal Inference and Mediation Analysis under Case-Control Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26543v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Population causal effects and their decomposition into direct and indirect effects cannot be estimated from case-control data without the outcome prevalence in the source population, which is rarely known. Because case-control sampling alters the population only through the outcome, the population is a mixture of the case and control distributions whose mixing weight is the prevalence. We show that this weight is identified by the population mean of any function of a single covariate that differs between cases and controls, and characterize what a reference distribution of the covariates identifies. This yields three prevalence estimators: moment calibration by the generalized method of moments, and two density-ratio fixed-point estimators using an individual-level reference sample or a synthetic sample drawn from the marginal distributions. Design weights based on the estimated prevalence reconstruct the population, and inverse probability weighting gives the total effect, the pure direct and indirect effects, and the mediated interaction. For the calibration estimator, we derive the joint asymptotic distribution of all stages and an overidentification test of the external information; we also quantify the sensitivity of the effects to the prevalence and extend the approach to frequency-matched designs. Simulations and an NHANES application illustrate the methods.</p></details> | 35 pages, 3 figures |
| **[Improving causal inference in interrupted time series analysis: the triple difference design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17281v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Background: Interrupted time series analysis (ITSA) is widely used to evaluate health policy and intervention effects. While multiple-group ITSA (MG-ITSA) improves causal inference by incorporating a control group, residual confounding from unmeasured time-varying factors may remain. The triple-difference interrupted time series (DDD-ITSA) design extends this approach by adding a second control group to further isolate treatment effects, but it remains underutilized and lacks formal guidance. Methods: We formalize the DDD-ITSA framework, specify the regression model, define key parameters for estimating level and trend effects, and clarify interpretation of the triple-difference estimand. We illustrate the approach using a worked example evaluating California's Proposition 99 cigarette tax and its impact on per-capita cigarette sales. Results: In the example, all groups were balanced on pre-intervention level and trend. The triple-difference estimand indicated a statistically significant annual reduction of -1.76 per-capita cigarette packs in California relative to the secondary control (P = 0.020; 95 percent CI: -3.24, -0.28), consistent with results from the primary comparison. Differences between control groups were not significant. Conclusions: DDD-ITSA strengthens causal inference when two-group comparisons may be confounded by leveraging an additional control group to remove remaining biases and assess heterogeneity. Implementation is facilitated by updates to the itsa Stata package. Careful attention to control selection, baseline balance, and autocorrelation remains essential.</p></details> |  |
| **[Not Just Why, but When and for Whom: Revisiting Common Estimands in Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36084v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Causal inference commonly defines an individual treatment effect as a quantity that varies across units. But we know such effects, even for a fixed unit, can also vary over time, across repeated studies, or over different methods of measurement, and they can even exhibit inherent stochasticity. In this work, we consider how such additional sources of variation create a need for more general notions of average treatment effects, including some estimands that have yet to be more formally explored by the causal inference community. This perspective also motivates a reconceptualization of our most common existing estimands, such as conditional average, sample average, and individual treatment effects (CATEs, SATEs, and ITEs), as subject to more axes of variation than are typically considered. We explore two main consequences of this reframing in the case where we have data from a single randomized controlled trial: (1) decision making in fixed samples and (2) uncertainty quantification during estimation. We find that different estimands, even for the same unit or the same sample, can qualitatively disagree on whether an effect is positive or negative. We demonstrate these issues empirically through a case study with real data and characterize through a simulation study how our assumptions about these sources of variation, even if we cannot observe the variation, can be consequential for estimation and proper coverage performance. Importantly, these phenomena arise even in the simplest of cases, where all structural and parametric assumptions are satisfied. More broadly, this paper aims to provide a preliminary investigation into an underexplored class of assumptions --- assumptions about unobserved non-confounders --- that should be considered when estimating treatment effects.</p></details> |  |

On four named accidents, abduction gives a probability of necessity of 0.29 to 0.50 for the heat omission, under monotonicity. For design-independent base weights under proportional asymptotics, we characterise how limiting risk depends on source and target covariance geometry, population mean shift, and weight concentration. For covariate-adaptive base weights, we develop a uniformly consistent target-aware risk estimator whose minimiser attains vanishing scaled oracle excess risk. Simulations show that the high-dimensional risk predictions remain informative for adaptive balancing and that target-aware tuning generally reduces excess target risk. Empirical analyses of job-training and single-cell perturbation data show that risk-calibrated balancing generally improves on the corresponding base estimators, with larger gains under weaker overlap.</p></details> |  |
| **[Invariance of Clustering Operations in Causal Effect Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03101v1)** | 2026-10-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clustering variables in causal graphs reduces the size of the graph and simplifies causal inference. We present a broad class of clustering operations that are identification invariant based on conditions related to the c-components of the original graph. | **[Can LLMs Uncover Hidden Physical Laws through Free Exploration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37649v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific discovery often begins with an unexpected observation that prompts a new research question. In a world with altered physical laws, can large language models (LLMs) notice unexpected behavior, such as heavier objects falling more slowly, and discover the law through experiments without being told what to investigate? To evaluate this ability, we introduce LongChang, an automated framework that creates simulated worlds with altered physics, where one or more laws differ from ordinary physics. The evaluated LLMs act as explorers, manipulating objects and building tools to investigate their own questions without research tasks or hints about these laws. Separate LLM agents generate and review scenes, review tool designs, and evaluate reports for correctness and experimental support using the altered physical laws and exploration records. These records guide scene evolution for further exploration, allowing the full cycle to run without human intervention. Among 12 LLMs, GPT-6-Astra achieves a discovery rate of 16.7%, while the other 11 score 0%. The exploration records reveal uninvestigated anomalies and only partial identification of the altered physical laws.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://zhenyangcs.github.io/LongChang-website/</p></details> |
| **[Rare, Relevant, and Still Wrong: How Memory Fails LLM Group Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37624v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM group's discussion is compressed into a short memory for a final decision maker, what should the memory keep? Two natural heuristics keep the rarest claims or the claims relevant to the decision criteria. On hidden-profile tasks both look attractive, because how widely a fact is held is confounded with whether it helps. We introduce CrossProfile, a diagnostic benchmark of 60 templates with automatically verified answer keys, in which the ownership of supporting, decoy, and irrelevant facts is crossed while their text is held fixed (480 tasks). Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and two DeepSeek models, both heuristics fail in predictable places. Selecting by relevance readmits decoys, because decoys are on topic: even a perfect relevance filter loses 23 to 31 points to rarest-first selection in the classic hidden-profile configuration. Rarest-first selection is no better than random selection on average and wins only where the rare facts happen to be the useful ones. In both cases accuracy follows the balance of evidence the memory keeps, not the policy that kept it. The decision model adds a third failure, a form of social proof: it gives extra weight to facts attributed to many agents. Removing the attribution tags, or reassigning them at random with line order fixed, raises accuracy in the classic configuration by 13 to 24 points. In natural discussions the evidence is lost before any memory acts: the complete transcript yields lower accuracy than no notes at all.</p></details> |  |

Accepted at ECML PKDD 2026 (Industrial Track)</p></details> |

On 1,152 optimized CORE attacks across six Amazon product categories and six MS MARCO web domains, SCI-Defense detects 89.6% of reasoning attacks and 96.2% of review attacks at a 1.2% false positive rate and blocks 96.1% of the attacks that pushed their target into the top three. Probabilistic approaches, such as semantic retrieval or text-based diffing, fail to capture the structural ripple effects across highly interconnected design dependencies. We propose a paradigm shift from probabilistic semantic retrieval to deterministic structural constraints. We formalize the design assets as a Knowledge Graph and organize them into a content-addressed Merkle Directed Acyclic Graph. Our key insight reveals a topological isomorphism between the logical ripple effect of a design change and the content hash propagation within the DAG. By leveraging this, we transform ambiguous impact analysis into verifiable structural operations. Through a two-phase diffing process, we extract a precise causal chain that defines the exact modification boundary. This boundary enables deterministic context injection, providing the LLM agent with strictly scoped code and design updates, thereby fundamentally guaranteeing evolution safety. As a by-product, the KG serves as an agent-queryable knowledge base for natural language design retrieval. Early deployment on a large-scale industrial payment platform demonstrates that our mechanism successfully computes the exact impact boundary and generates incremental code modifications that meet the expected design changes.</p></details> |  |

| **[The Inbox as a Coordination Primitive for LLM Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36264v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents increasingly coordinate in large groups, and current agent protocols focus on delivering messages. For these agents, however, the scarce resource is not delivery but reading: every message costs tokens, and the context is finite. Current infrastructure works by push: the sender decides when a message enters the recipient's context, so the recipient reads in arrival order and drops the oldest messages once its context is full. We study the alternative, pull, in which messages wait in an inbox outside the context and the recipient's own rules decide what it reads and keeps. We argue that the inbox is a coordination primitive: each agent sets its rules privately, and those rules decide whether other agents' requests reach it. In simulated societies of up to 16,000 agents, with every decision an LLM judgment, we report three findings. First, push fails as an agent takes part in more coordination at once: an agent leading 20 negotiations with a full memory proposes options already rejected 97% of the time, because it has dropped the oldest replies. Second, an inbox is necessary under heavy, noisy traffic. Where first come, first read misses every request, an inbox that first reads messages carrying a refundable deposit still finds about 60% of the agents a request needs, at every size from 1,000 to 16,000. On a real email network, losses compound: when half the agents adopt such an inbox, forwarded search recovers only 5% of full adoption, against 42% for one-hop search. Third, an inbox comprises four decisions (what to read, what to keep, what a sender pays, and what a reply may say), and the best rule for each depends on the traffic and the agent's role. | **[Context Is a Pipeline, Not a Window: Information Attrition in LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36114v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are judged by two numbers: the size of the context window, and how often the final answer is correct. | **[Adversary Brains: Hypothesis-Based Logical Signatures for Threat Actor Detection with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35624v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An adversary brain is an LLM-based model of a threat actor's behavior, held as an evolving logical signature: a hypothesis with enough structure that data can test it continuously, without a person re-interpreting it each time. | **[GEPA-Curator: Curated Learning for Self Improving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37651v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in multi-turn agentic workloads that require sustained interaction with complex environments. Existing reflective prompt optimizers such as GEPA learn from failed rollouts, but agentic failures can also come from prompt weaknesses, harness limitations, stochastic execution, or noisy judges. We argue that such failures may not provide useful learning signal, and may even be harmful; and thus it is essential to curate which tasks are provided to the prompt optimizer for better performance. We introduce GEPA-Curator, a framework that augments the prompt optimizer to select which agent experiences should shape prompt optimization. In particular, GEPA-Curator consists of two components: task curation, which filters out tasks that are likely to provide noisy or unreliable learning signals, and prior success retrieval, which selectively retrieves past successful trajectories for a task and provides them as additional context to the prompt optimizer. We evaluate GEPA-Curator on GoBrowse, ALFWorld, AppWorld, and $τ$-bench, using both Gemini 3.1 Pro and Claude Opus 5 as optimizer models. GEPA-Curator matches or exceeds the mean performance of both baselines, with gains of up to 20.7% over GEPA and 21.2% over ACE.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scaling Laws for Tool-Use Agents via Controllable Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37648v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We explore how to build performant large language model (LLM) tool-use agents that complete complex, multi-step tasks. To do so, we propose the SynthAPI pipeline, which explicitly controls the diversity of generated environments along the axes of tools, tasks, and the databases on which the tools operate. By systematically varying SynthAPI's generation controls in 458,752 tasks across 849 trained models of four different sizes, we derive a scaling law that predicts downstream tool-use agent generalization based on the scale and composition of the synthesized environments. These predictable relationships reveal several lessons for scaling tool-use agent data. As data budgets grow, scaling tool and database diversity becomes more effective than increasing the number of tasks, and increasing data complexity yields larger gains. Scaling according to these findings yields agents that outperform models trained on data from prior work in large-scale tool-use agent and synthetic environment generation across four evaluation benchmarks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Metacognitive Decoding: Efficient Inference for Diffusion Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37642v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Diffusion language models offer a promising path to efficient agents through parallel token generation. However, efficient interaction requires adapting both where computation is spent and how much text is generated. We introduce a metacognitive controller that guides diffusion generation according to the needs of the current response and the agent's interaction with the environment. It learns when to finalize tokens and how far generation should extend, reducing overall computation within responses. Our controller, a lightweight plug-in for the diffusion backbone, is trained on agent trajectories to balance correctness and generation cost. This approach connects the agent's generation process to the interaction the agent serves. Experiments across five benchmarks demonstrate that our approach reduces inference FLOPs by 20.6% on average compared with the most efficient baseline, while maintaining competitive task accuracy. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of our approach.</p></details> | 10 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Annotrace: A Workbench for Step-Level Annotation of Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23284v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every study of why LLM agents fail begins by labelling traces by hand, and every such study builds its own throwaway tool. Observability platforms record agent traces but offer no structured labelling; annotation tools label text but have no notion of a step, a delegation or a tool call, leaving it unclear what the smallest unit of execution to label should be. Annotrace reads six trace formats, including OpenTelemetry GenAI, LangSmith and Langfuse, and turns each run into an ordered, nested list of steps. The annotator writes their own labelling codebook; a frontier model proposes a starting set from a sample of their own traces, answers first, and is asked exactly the questions the human is asked, making annotation a joint human-AI process. Annotrace measures agreement between any two annotators, human or AI. On a multi-agent question-answering corpus it reproduces a previous manual effort's 235 annotation units exactly, and two models labelling the same traces agree 91% of the time at $κ= 0.82$.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 4 figures, 1 table. Under review at the AAAI 2027 Demonstration Program</p></details> |
| **[Agentic Distributional Regression: Conditional Density Estimation through Agent-Written Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37611v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper we propose a method for estimating conditional densities from an iid sample using a language-model agent. The agent writes the estimator as a short program that, for each covariate value, returns the estimated conditional distribution of the response. The program is built from a library of probability distributions, so the returned law can be a parametric distribution, a mixture or a multivariate factorization, with parameters given by explicit functions of the covariates. Since the number of operations and constants in the program is limited, the fitted conditional density is a formula that can be inspected and evaluated independently of the agent, while the choice of the model structure and of the fitting procedure is left to the agent. We call this approach agentic distributional regression (ADR). Treating the returned program as a random conditional density estimator, we derive a finite-sample error bound under explicit complexity and optimization assumptions, and an oracle inequality for selection using independent validation data. For a stably selected regular structure with sufficiently accurate fitting, we obtain asymptotic normality of the fitted constants. In simulation studies, on twelve real datasets and on a new dataset collected after the agents' training data, ADR with Claude Opus 5.5 has the best average performance among the methods considered.</p></details> |  |
| **[EnvSched: Environment-Aware Scheduling for Rollout-Efficient Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37609v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As executable environments for language agents scale, generating and verifying their rollouts becomes increasingly expensive. To address this question, we introduce EnvSched (Environment-aware Scheduling), an online scheduler that learns from rollout history. EnvSched assigns scheduling value through two complementary modeling choices: exploitation prioritizes tasks likely to provide informative reward differences, while coverage spreads each batch across underobserved environments and interaction patterns. EnvSched organizes environment--task pairs as nodes in a dual-relation graph, with within-environment edges capturing task semantics and shared tool, state, and verifier structure, and cross-environment edges capturing abstract execution and reward topology. By propagating historical rollout statistics along both edge types, it predicts within-task reward variance. These predictions guide a scheduler that allocates each batch to tasks with high predicted reward variance and underobserved environments and tasks. Environment quotas and a similarity penalty further limit concentration and redundancy, yielding informative and diverse batches without additional pilot rollouts. In RL on Qwen3-8B, EnvSched achieves an average score of 48.81 across BFCL-v3, tau-Bench, and ACEBench-Agent after 40 training steps, exceeding the highest score attained by random task selection after 120 steps. | **[Detailed balance in large language model-driven agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10047v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-driven agents are emerging as a powerful new paradigm for solving complex problems. By experimentally measuring the transition probabilities between LLM-generated states, we report evidence consistent with a tendency toward detailed balance in LLM-generated transitions, indicating that LLM generation may not be achieved by generally learning rule sets and strategies, but rather by implicitly learning a class of underlying potential functions that may transcend different LLM architectures and prompt templates. To our knowledge, this provides an effective macroscopic description of LLM generative dynamics that does not depend on specific model details. This work is an attempt to establish a macroscopic dynamics theory of complex AI systems, aiming to elevate the study of AI agents from a collection of engineering practices to a science built on effective measurements that are predictable and quantifiable.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 19 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[TANDEM: Balancing Centralized and Decentralized Critics to Address the Information Gap in Complex Multi-Agent Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37592v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning under centralized training with decentralized execution (CTDE) shapes decentralized policies with a centralized critic that observes the global state, then removes the critic at execution. The policies are optimized to rely on information they will not have when they act, an information gap between training and execution. It widens under partial observability, observation noise, and openness, where the set of agents and tasks present changes during an episode. Existing methods transfer centralized guidance in one direction only, removing or distilling the critic once, so the policies never rehearse acting without the critic while they can still be corrected. We propose TANDEM, which keeps one centralized critic and reads it two ways at each training step: a centralized read from the global state, and a decentralized read restricted to the information each agent will have at execution. A parameter-free gate compares the two reads for each agent and trains the policy on the decentralized read where they agree, reinstating the centralized read only where the local view is misleading. A shared target pulls the decentralized read toward the centralized one. We formalize the setting as a task- and agent-open decentralized partially observable Markov decision process (TAO-Dec-POMDP) and evaluate TANDEM on three cooperative domains, each isolating one form of the information gap: discovery for partial observability, sampling for observation noise, and openness in wildfire suppression. We compare against six CTDE baselines and ablate the gate and the shared target; the supplementary material holds the full variant sweep, diagnostics, and learning curves. We measure return at execution, once the centralized critic is removed. TANDEM is the best method on eight of the nine setups and never significantly worse than any baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[TeamGym: A Gym for Team Decision-Making by AI Agents with Distributed Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37571v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many real-world decisions require teams to integrate information distributed across participants. Examining 8 language models on 12 source corpora, we find that current AI agents often fail to effectively share information: Distributing pivotal information across participants lowers success rate by 22.9 percentage points on average relative to giving the same information to a single model (a communication gap). | **[Gym-Anything-RT: Scaling Real-Time Environments for Vision-Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37567v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents have made substantial progress in domains where they have a non-trivial amount of time to deliberate between actions, such as mathematics, software engineering, and performing tasks on desktop applications. It is less clear how such models fare in settings that demand real-time interaction, such as playing a video game or operating a physical device. To gain insight into the real-time interaction capabilities of vision-language agents, we introduce Gym-Anything-RT. Our key idea is to construct visual minigames that demand a portfolio of capabilities required for interactive real-time settings. A key feature of Gym-Anything-RT is that it allows for controlled comparison of real-time interaction capabilities: we can run the same minigame in real time, or pause or slow down the environment to relax the requirement for real-time actions. | **[EvoIn: Bridging Evolution and Internalization for Agent Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35290v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work has explored improving agents by jointly evolving their harnesses and models, but often takes a ''potpourri'' approach that bundles together new tools, new decision-making procedures, and model adaptation to the evolved harness under a single notion of agent improvement. In particular, we propose EvoIn, an agent fine-tuning framework that bridges evolution and internalization. EvoIn first analyzes agent execution traces to evolve and validate new decision-making procedures by temporarily instantiating them in the harness. The validated procedures guide the agent to generate improved reasoning traces. These traces are then rewritten into self-contained reasoning traces, removing explicit references to harness instructions while expressing the induced decision logic as the model's own reasoning. Finally, EvoIn fine-tunes the model on the rewritten traces, internalizing these procedures so that the improved decision-making persists without the evolved harness at inference time. We evaluate EvoIn on diverse benchmarks and find that it consistently enables agents to learn stronger decision-making procedures, raising the pass rate by 10.9 points in-domain and by 9.2 points out-of-domain. Results further show that the internalized decision procedures generalize to unseen tasks. Case studies show that agents can learn to decide how to solve a task before solving it, for example by checking a document's length to choose between reading it in full and searching it.
